c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m}}(A)\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\frac{({-}1)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m}!}\left|\begin{array}[]{@{}cccccc@{}}\mathrm{tr}({A})&1&0&\cdots&0\\
\mathrm{tr}({A}^{2})&\mathrm{tr}({A})&2&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
\mathrm{tr}({A}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m}{-}1})&\ddots&\ddots&\mathrm{tr}({A})&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m}{-}1\\
\mathrm{tr}({A}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m}})&\mathrm{tr}({A}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m}{-}1})&\cdots&\mathrm{tr}({A}^{2})&\mathrm{tr}({A})\end{array}\right|\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.21)

This identity does not immediately suggest anything about traces built from ordered products of generators; but we may apply this identity to the matrix
A\!\mapsto\!\sum_{a{=}1}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}T_{\mathbf{F}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma(a)}}
, which would then involve the symmetrized products of subsets of generators chosen from the list indexed by
\sigma(a)
.

Because we are interested in
({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}1})
-particle scattering, we may contract this identity with any additional generator and take its trace, resulting in a new identity among the traces involving the
({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}1})
generators.

Consider the case of
\mathfrak{a}_{2}
. We’d like to find an identity among the 4-particle multi-traces of the
\mathbf{3}
-dimensional generators of the fundamental representation. In this case, and using the fact that the generator matrices are traceless (true for any representation of any semi-simple Lie algebra), the identity (
3.20
) takes the form:

A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}}{+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}}(A)A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}}{+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}}(A)A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}}{+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}}(A)A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0}}=0\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(3.22)

with coefficients given by

\begin{split}c_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}(A)&={-}\mathrm{tr}(A)=0\,;\\
c_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}(A)&=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(A^{1})^{2}{-}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}})={-}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}})\,;\\
c_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}(A)&=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}})\mathrm{tr}(A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}}){-}\frac{1}{6}\mathrm{tr}(A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}})^{3}{-}\frac{1}{3}\mathrm{tr}(A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}})={-}\frac{1}{3}\mathrm{tr}(A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}})\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.23)

Relative to the general case, the only simplification arising here is due to the vanishing of
\mathrm{tr}(A)
.

Contracting the identity (
3.22
) on the left with any
other
(traceless) matrix
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}
, and taking the trace of the result leads to the identity

\begin{split}0&=\mathrm{tr}\big[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}.\!\left(A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}}{+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}}(A)A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}}{+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}}(A)A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}}{+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}}(A)A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0}}\right)\!\big]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}.A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}}\big){+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}}(A)\mathrm{tr}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}.A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2}}\big){+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}}(A)\mathrm{tr}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}.A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}}\big){+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}}(A)\mathrm{tr}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B})\\
&=\mathrm{tr}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}.A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}}\big){+}c_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}}(A)\mathrm{tr}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}.A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}}\big)\\
&=\mathrm{tr}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}.A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3}}\big){-}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(A^{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2})\mathrm{tr}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}.A^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1}}\big)\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.24)

Replacing
A
with the sum over any subset of the
\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{a}_{2}){=}8
generators and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}\!\mapsto T^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}a}}_{\mathbf{F}}
any other will result in an identity

\hskip-20.0pt\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}(\{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2},{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3},{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}4}\})}\hskip-10.0pt\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{3}}){=}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{1}})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{2}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{3}}){+}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{2}})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{3}}){+}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{3}})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{1}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}\sigma_{2}})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.25)

This generalizes in a nice way to all
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
: for
({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}1})
-point scattering of adjoints in
\mathfrak{a}_{n{-}1}
gauge theory, there exists a single identity:

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{distinct}\\
\text{multi-traces}\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.0pt({-}1)^{|\mathrm{tr}_{i}|}\mathrm{tr}_{i}=0\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.26)

This agrees with the empirical observation that
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}1}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{n{-}1}}{=}\,\,!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}1}){+}1
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\geq 2
(see
Table
12
).

When the rank is lower and the size of the generators of the fundamental representation is substantially smaller than a multiplicity in question, there will be additional identities arising from replacing ‘
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}
’ in the argument above with products over more generators. Choosing different (non-symmetrized) sets of generators chosen for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}B}
gives rise to many (not necessarily independent) identities.

The same logic applies to the cases of
\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}
-type gauge theory below saturation. The main distinction is that for these algebras saturation always occurs when the dimension of the fundamental representation is farther from the multiplicity than the marginal case encountered for
\mathfrak{a}
-series:

\left.\begin{array}[]{lr}\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{b}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}},\mathfrak{c}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}}=\,(!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})&\text{for }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}>\frac{1}{2}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{+}1)\\
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{b}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}},\mathfrak{c}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}}}<\,(!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})&\text{for }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}\leq\frac{1}{2}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{+}1)\end{array}\right.\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(3.27)

This explains a greater number of identities among the tensors away from saturation.

The above discussion helps to explain the decrease in the counting of independent tensors only because we considered multi-traces over the fundamental representation—which involves matrices small enough for Cayley-Hamilton’s relation to apply. But what if we had chosen another representation? For example, although we have identified an identity among the
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{=}{6}
-particle multi-traces over the generators of the
\mathbf{5}
-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{su}_{5}
, what if we had instead chosen a basis of multi-traces over the generators of the
\mathbf{10}
-dimensional representation? Of course, the number of independent colour-tensors has nothing to do with the basis in which we choose to represent them; but it is clear that the logic above seems strongly sensitive to the
smallest
-dimensional representation that exists for a given algebra. It would be worthwhile to better understand how this story changes if other representations are used.

3.2.2
There be
\mathfrak{d}
ragons: Origins of non-Monotonicity for
\mathfrak{d}
-Theories

The
\mathfrak{d}_{k}
series is remarkable because for
low rank
there are
extra
colour-tensors relative to the large-
k
limit; as
k
increases, these additional tensors must become dependent (or cease to exist).

This should not be entirely surprising, however, as the
\mathfrak{d}
-type algebras (for odd rank) admit
complex
representations; as such, even if multi-traces over (real) fundamental generators should saturate to
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
(reflecting the dihedral symmetry of traces over any real representation), surely we’d expect fewer relations (and a larger basis) to be spanned by multi-traces over
complex
representations such as spinors. This argument is a red-herring: for one thing, not all
\mathfrak{d}
admit complex representations. Indeed, although no such simple pairwise relations as in (
3.16
) exist for multi-traces over the spinors of
\mathfrak{d}_{2k{+}1}
, we have checked explicitly that they span a space of rank
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
above the saturation limit.

We can understand the origin of these ‘new’ tensors looking just below the marginal limit. Consider, for example,
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}4}
-adjoint scattering in
\mathfrak{d}_{4}
-type gauge theory. As described in
section
2.1
, the generators of the fundamental representation can be represented by the collection of matrices
{{T}_{\mathbf{F}}}^{\hskip-5.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}(ij)}}_{\hskip-7.0pt\phantom{(ij)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r\,s}}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\delta^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}\delta^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}j}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}s}}{-}\delta^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}s}}\delta^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}j}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}r}}
indexed by
({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}ij})\!\in\!\binom{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2k}]}{2}
. For
k{=}4
, we will have 28 generators of size
8\!\times\!8
. We can think of each as a two-form, and consider the antisymmetric product of any
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}4}
of them to obtain an
8
-form; dividing this by the volume form (that is, contracting the representation indices with a determinant) results in a completely symmetric tensor in the adjoint indices related to the
Pfaffian
of the generators.

This object ceases to exist for
k\!>\!n
, and surely accounts for the
one
excess that
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}{=}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}}{=}(!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}){+}1
. Moreover, such a construction requires that the representation be even-dimensional, and therefore does not apply to the algebras
\mathfrak{b}_{k}\!\sim\!\mathfrak{so}_{2k{+}1}
.

Further below saturation, for
k\!<\!n
, there are even more possibilities. Indeed, such Pfaffian-like tensors can be constructed using subsets of generators according to the above. Consider the case of
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
-adjoint scattering in
\mathfrak{d}_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{-}1}
theory. In this case, we could construct Pfaffians using
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{-}1
of the generators, but there are no single-particle invariants available from which we may build an
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
-adjoint tensor. There is, however, an invariant 2-adjoint tensor (namely the Cartan-Killing form), and we could construct an
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
-adjoint tensor using the Pfaffian built from any choice of
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{-}2
of them wedged with the commutator.

Let us try to be a bit more concrete. To describe the generators, let us denote the colour-tensor in the fundamental representation for the particle
a
by
\underline{T}_{a}
. We can think of these
\underline{T}_{a}
as two-forms on the
2({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{-}1)
-dimensional space on which the representation acts. In addition to these two-forms, there is a two-form associated with the commutator
[\underline{T}_{a},\underline{T}_{b}]
. We can form Pfaffian-like invariants using the commutator as in
[\underline{T}_{a},\underline{T}_{b}]\wedge\underline{T}_{1}\!\wedge\!\cdots\!\wedge\widehat{\underline{T}}_{a}\!\wedge\!\cdots\!\wedge\widehat{\underline{T}}_{b}\!\wedge\!\cdots\!\wedge\!\underline{T}_{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
, where hats denote generators not appearing in the wedge-product. These tensors are not all independent, however: for every
a\!\in\![{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}]
, we have an identity
\sum_{b}[\underline{T}_{a},\underline{T}_{b}]\wedge\dots\widehat{\underline{T}}_{a}\dots\widehat{\underline{T}}_{b}\dots\wedge\underline{T}_{n}{=}0
, which is simply the invariance condition of
\underline{T}_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\widehat{\underline{T}}_{a}\wedge\dots\wedge\underline{T}_{n}
under a gauge transformation; but these relations are not themselves independent: they have one syzygy (their sum vanishes identically).

Thus, for every
\binom{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{2}
pair of indices we can construct a new invariant tensor; these satisfy
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
identities of which only
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{-}1
are independent. Thus, from this construction we expect to be able to build
\binom{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{2}
new tensors, spanning
\binom{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{2}{-}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{+}1{=}\binom{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{-}1}{2}
independent objects. Moreover, since these new invariants involve the Levi-Civita tensor they are invariants for the
special
orthogonal group; the other invariants which do not involve the Levi-Civita tensor are invariants for the
orthogonal
group and therefore they should be independent.

Indeed, this agrees with our explicit calculations. Consulting Table
15
, one can check that
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}8}
adjoints,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}8}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{9}}{=}6202{=}!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}8}
,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}8}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{8}}{=}6203{=}!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}8}{+}1
and for
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}8}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{7}}{=}6223{=}!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}8}{+}\binom{7}{2}.

3.3
Counting in the Limit of Large Multiplicity (for a Fixed Lie Algebra)

For any Lie algebra of fixed rank
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}
, however, there will be an unbounded range of
multiplicities
which are
outside
/above the saturation condition for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}
; and for the exceptional algebras, no notion of a ‘large rank limit’ exists. For any algebra at sufficiently low rank, even relatively low-multiplicity scattering will be outside saturation, and it is interesting to see how
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
compares with saturation in these instances. We have tabulated many of these numbers in
Appendix
C
and provided them also as an ancillary file attached to this work.

On general grounds, as we review in
Appendix
D
, it turns out that for any fixed Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
,

\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}\#\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}d}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}d}/2}\exp\!\left\{{-}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}d}^{2}}{48\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\right\}\,\Big(1{+}\mathcal{O}(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})\Big)\,\vskip-0.5pt

(3.28)

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}d}{=}\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})
and ‘
\#
’ is some fixed constant that can be computed for any algebra. (These constants have been tabulated for all algebras of rank
k\!\leq\!8
in
Appendix
D
.) This is steep growth indeed, but dramatically slower than the
factorial
growth of
!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
or
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
.

Figure 3
:
Comparison of
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
with the asymptotic formulae (
3.29
) with and without the
\mathcal{O}(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})
correction terms through reasonably high multiplicity.

In some cases, the
\mathcal{O}(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})
corrections are known or can be deduced from the data directly. For example,

\begin{split}\mathfrak{a}_{1}\!\!:\;\;\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}&=\frac{1}{8\sqrt{3\,\pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\exp\!\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}^{2}\hskip-3.0pt}{\hskip 1.0pt48\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\,\right\}\Big(1{-}\frac{9}{8\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{+}\mathcal{O}\!\big(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{2}\big)\Big)\;;\\
\mathfrak{a}_{2}\!\!:\;\;\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{2}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}&=\frac{4}{81\sqrt{3}\,\pi}\hskip 0.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}8}^{\hskip-0.25pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\text{+}2}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}8}}{2}}\exp\!\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}8}^{2}\hskip-3.0pt}{\hskip 1.0pt48\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\,\right\}\Big(\!1{-}\frac{14}{3\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{+}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{2}\right)\!\!\Big)\;;\\
\mathfrak{b}_{2}\!\!:\;\;\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{b}_{2}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}&=\frac{125}{1296\,\pi}\hskip 0.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}10}^{\hskip-0.25pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\text{+}2}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}10}}{2}}\exp\!\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}10}^{2}\hskip-3.0pt}{\hskip 1.0pt48\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\,\right\}\Big(\!1{-}\frac{20}{3\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{+}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{2}\right)\!\!\Big)\,;\\
\mathfrak{g}_{2}\!\!:\;\;\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{2}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}&=\frac{84035}{442368\sqrt{3}\,\pi}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}14}^{\hskip-0.25pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\text{+}2}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}14}}{2}}\exp\!\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}14}^{2}\hskip-3.0pt}{\hskip 1.0pt48\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\,\right\}\Big(\!1{-}\frac{35}{3\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{+}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{2}\right)\!\!\Big)\,;\\
\mathfrak{a}_{3}\!\!:\;\;\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{3}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}&=\frac{3}{2^{21}\pi^{3/2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}15}^{\hskip-0.25pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\text{+}15/2}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}15}}{2}}\exp\!\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}15}^{2}\hskip-3.0pt}{\hskip 1.0pt48\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\,\right\}\Big(\!1{-}\frac{105}{8\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}{+}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{2}\right)\!\!\Big)\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(3.29)

In
Figure
3
, we show how direct computation of
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
compares with the asymptotic formulae both with and without the
\mathcal{O}(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})
corrections.

3.3.1
Surveys of Asymptotic Growth at Moderately Large Multiplicity

For any modest-rank Lie algebra
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
can be computed reasonably efficiently through
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\!\sim\!\mathcal{O}(100)
(and in some instances, much higher) using readily available computer algebra packages (such as
[
30
,
31
]
or the
Mathematica
package of
[
32
]
). We have tabulated many of these results in
Appendix
C
, and included precise ranks to reasonably-high multiplicity for all rank
k\!\leq\!8
algebras in the ancillary file attached to this work. We plot these numbers relative to their saturated values for a given multiplicity in
Figure
5
.

Figure 4
:
Relative numbers of independent colour-tensors for
\mathbf{ad}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}
scattering in
\mathfrak{c}_{k}
vs.
\mathfrak{b}_{k}
.

The similarity between the counting for
\mathfrak{b}_{k}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{k}
gauge theories is striking. Although the counting in both cases saturate to
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
, below saturation (at higher
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
for fixed rank) there are slightly fewer colour-tensors for
\mathfrak{c}_{k}
gauge theories than
\mathfrak{b}_{k}
theories. In fact, our analysis above suggests that the ratio
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{c}_{k}}/\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{b}_{k}}
goes to a constant as
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\!\to\!\infty
for any fixed
k
. This is indeed seen in the explicit data, as indicated in
Figure
4
, with the asymptotic ratios predicted by the asymptotic formulae given in
Appendix
D
indicated on the right for
k{=}3,4,5
.

Although there is no large-rank limit for Lie algebras of type
\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}
, we have plotted the counting
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
against
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
as only
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
admits complex representations. The sequences that arise for
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{e}_{6}}
,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{e}_{7}}
,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{e}_{8}}
,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{f}_{4}}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{2}}
are quite interesting—indeed, they are enshrined in the OEIS
[
33
]
as
[
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
,
38
]
, respectively.

Because
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
’s fundamental representation is not self-conjugate, it should not be terribly surprising that
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{e}_{6}}/(!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})
exceeds 1 for a limited range of multiplicities (with a maximum occurring, suggestively, at
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{=}27
); to be clear
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}}/(!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})\!<\!1
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
, which is consistent with an always over-complete basis consisting of multi-traces over the fundamental representation’s generators. However, we have not been able to construct explicit these for
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{=}19
, say, to directly verify that multi-traces over the fundamental representation
\mathbf{27}
truly span the space of
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}19}}_{\mathfrak{e}_{6}}{=}12\,702\,789\,216\,958\,134
independent tensors.

At low multiplicity, these algebras have among the fewest independent colour-tensors. In particular,
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
gauge theory requires the fewest of any algebra other than
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\!<\!10
. For
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{=}4
, this is true for all the exceptional algebras, each of which have only
5{=}\,(!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}4}){-}1
independent tensors, which we can understand through the relations (which we have checked directly):

\mathfrak{e}_{6}\!:\;\;12\,\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2\,3\,4}){=}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3\,4})

\mathfrak{f}_{4}\!:\;\;12\,\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2\,3\,4}){=}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3\,4})

\mathfrak{e}_{7}\!:\;\;24\,\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2\,3\,4}){=}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3\,4})

\mathfrak{g}_{2}\!:\;\;4\,\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2\,3\,4}){=}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3\,4})

\mathfrak{e}_{8}\!:\;\;100\,\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2\,3\,4}){=}\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}(1\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2})\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbf{F}}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}3\,4})\,;

(3.30)

here, indices coloured red should be cyclically-symmetrized. Notice that applying this logic to the case of
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
only works because, although its fundamental representation is
not
real, it is nevertheless true that its traces enjoy reflection symmetry through length 5 (and not beyond)—a fact which accounts for the absence of a
d^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}a\,b\,c}}
tensor for
\mathfrak{e}_{6}

[
4
]
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\raisebox{-100.0pt}{\includegraphics[scale={1}]{relativeColourCountingPlotsA}}\hskip 15.0pt\raisebox{-100.0pt}{\includegraphics[scale={1}]{relativeColourCountingPlotsB}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\\[110.0pt]
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\raisebox{-100.0pt}{\includegraphics[scale={1}]{relativeColourCountingPlotsC}}\hskip 15.0pt\raisebox{-100.0pt}{\includegraphics[scale={1}]{relativeColourCountingPlotsD}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\\[110.0pt]
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\raisebox{-100.0pt}{\includegraphics[scale={1}]{relativeColourCountingPlotsEFG}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\end{array}

Figure 5
:
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
relative to saturation numbers for all Lie algebras through rank
8
.

4
Scattering of Gluons and Variously Charged Matter

As soon as we turn our attention beyond the adjoint representation, there are never-ending combinations to be analyzed. It would be impossible to concisely describe all the combinations we have explored, and we do not even claim to have exhausted the ‘interesting’ cases. Part of the purpose of what we describe here is to invite the reader to play these games themselves. Besides any particular relevance to physics these surveys may have, we find a wealth of connections to interesting combinatoric structure, and it is reasonable to expect some of the sequences we have not yet identified may have an interesting and rich structure.

4.1
Counting the Colours of Gluons with
Fundamental
-Charged Matter

Let us begin our studies with the case of a single type of matter particle charged under the representation
\mathbf{R}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. For the scattering of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}
fermion lines and
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
gluons, the number of independent colour-tensors would be computed by

\mathcal{C}\big[\mathbf{ad}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}},(\mathbf{R}\overline{\mathbf{R}})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}\big]\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\,\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}(\mathbf{R})=m\big(\mathbf{ad}^{\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\!\otimes\!(\mathbf{R}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{R}})^{\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}\!\!\to\!\mathbf{1}\big)\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.1)

It is a general fact that for any (non-trivial) irreducible representation, the tensor product
\mathbf{R}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{R}}
decomposes into irreducible representations according to

\mathbf{R}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!(\cdots)\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.2)

If we denote the combinations of irreducible representations (possibly with multiplicity) encoded by the ‘
(\cdots)
’ above by ‘
\mathbf{Q}
’, we see on general grounds (by the associativity of the tensor product) that

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{R})&=\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q{-}1}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{R})\!+\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q{-}1}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{R}){+}m\big(\mathbf{ad}^{\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\!\!\otimes\!(\mathbf{R}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{R}})^{\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q{-}1}}\!\!\otimes\!\mathbf{Q}\!\to\!\mathbf{1}\big)\,\\
&\geq\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q{-}1}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{R})\!+\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q{-}1}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{R})\,.\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(4.3)

In particular, for the fundamental representations (defined in
Table
1
), we have
9
9

9

Although one should generally avoid naming irreducible representations by their dimension alone, there are no ambiguities among those that appear here.

\begin{split}\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{a}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\\
\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{b}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big[}\mathbf{k(2k{+}3)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big]}\\
\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{c}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big[}\mathbf{(k{-}1)(2k{+}1)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big]}\\
\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{d}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}k}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big[}\mathbf{(k{+}1)(2k{-}1)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big]}\\
\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{e}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}6}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big[}\mathbf{650}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big]}\\
\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{e}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}7}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big[}\mathbf{1463}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{1539}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big]}\\
\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{e}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}8}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big[}\mathbf{3875}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{27000}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{30380}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big]}\\
\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}4}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big[}\mathbf{F}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{273}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{324}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big]}\\
\text{\makebox[20.0pt][l]{$\mathfrak{g}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}}\!\!:\;\;$}}&\mathbf{F}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{F}}=\mathbf{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{ad}\!\oplus\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big[}\mathbf{F}\!\oplus\!\mathbf{27}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}\big]}\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(4.4)

4.1.1
Scattering of Gluons and
Fundamental
Matter in
\mathfrak{a}
-Theories

Notice in particular that for the fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
, the remainder ‘
\mathbf{Q}
’ does not exist. This is in fact the only irreducible representation of any Lie algebra which has this property, and leads to a very simple recurrence:

\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{k}}(\mathbf{F})=\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q{-}1}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{k}}(\mathbf{F}){+}\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q{-}1}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{k}}(\mathbf{F})\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(4.5)

which is easily solved:

\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{k}}(\mathbf{F})=\sum_{m{=}0}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}\binom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}{m}\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}m}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{k}}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.6)

Let us consider the simplest example of charged matter in any non-abelian gauge theory: the fundamental (‘
\mathbf{2}
’) of
\mathfrak{su}_{2}
. The resulting numbers of independent colour-tensors are given in
Table
3
.

Table 3
:
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}(\mathbf{F})
: Scattering
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}\!\times\!\mathbf{2}
-charged lines and

Adjoints(
\mathbf{3}
s) in
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
gauge theory.

Notice that for
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{=}0
,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}(\mathbf{F})
is simply the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}
th
Catalan
number
[
39
]
! The second row is easily identified as the Motzkin numbers
[
40
]
. Each column of
Table
3
is obtained by subtracting successive entries of the previous column. As such, we may call this a ‘
Catalan difference table
’.

The recursive structure obviously holds for all cases of fundamental matter in
\mathfrak{a}
-type gauge theories. In particular, it holds for the saturated case obtained in the limit of large rank. Defining

!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}r},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\!\sum_{m{=}0}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}\,\binom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}{m}!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n{+}m})\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(4.7)

we may tabulate the large-
k
limit of
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{k}}(\mathbf{F})
directly. These numbers are given in
Table
4
. We already knew that in the large-
k
limit,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}0}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}}(\mathbf{F})=!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
, but we can further read off from the table that
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}}(\mathbf{F})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}!
. Indeed, starting from either of these one may recursively fill in the rest of the table, leading to what is sometimes called
Euler’s difference table
, as given in
Table
4
.

Table 4
:
!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})\text{: large-$k$ limit of scattering ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}\!\times\!\mathbf{F}$-charged lines and ${\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\times$adjoints in $\mathfrak{a}_{k}$-theory.}

These saturation numbers
!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})
provide a convenient reference-point in plotting the numbers of colour-tensors away from saturation (or for the exceptional algebras, where no notion of saturation exists). They are associated with the following exponential generating function in two variables:

!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\underset{!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})}{\text{EGF}}({x},y)\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}\frac{(!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}))}{({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}!)({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}!)}x^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}y^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}=\frac{e^{-{x}}}{1{-}{x}{-}y}.\,\vskip-0.5pt

(4.8)

Also note that
!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})\!=\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}!)
and
!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})\!=\!({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}^{2}{+}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}{+}1)({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}!)
.

4.1.2
Scattering of Gluons and
Fundamental
Matter in
\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{d}
-Theories

Outside of the case of the fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
, there is always some contribution(s) to
\mathbf{R}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{R}}
besides
\mathbf{1}
and
\mathbf{ad}
. This prevents us from repeating the simple recursive construction of the
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
case: generally speaking, knowledge of the number of independent adjoint tensors is not sufficient to recursively construct the numbers for some alternative particle content. Nevertheless, we may calculate these numbers directly in the large-rank limit (where
\mathfrak{b}_{k},\mathfrak{c}_{k},\mathfrak{d}_{k}
are again found to saturate identically) and collect the results in Table
5
. We will again generalize the adjoint notation
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
to
!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})
in the obvious way (
defined
as the entries in Table
5
). Amusingly,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}0}}_{(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{d})_{\infty}}(\mathbf{F})=!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
and
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{d})_{\infty}}(\mathbf{F})=(2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}-1)!!
, justifying the notation.
10
10

10

Also note that
({-}1)!!{=}1
is well-defined due to the recurrence
n!!{=}n(n{-}2)!!

In spite of the lack of a recurrence and the diverse nature of the
\mathfrak{b}_{k}/\mathfrak{c}_{k}/\mathfrak{d}_{k}
algebras, remarkably it turns out these numbers are
again
captured by a simple exponential generating function:

!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})\,\,\Leftrightarrow\,\,\underset{!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})}{\text{EGF}}({x},y)\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}\frac{(!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}))}{({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}!)({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}!)}x^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}y^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}=\frac{e^{{x}^{2}/4{-}x/2}}{\sqrt{1{-}{x}{-}2y}}.\,\vskip-0.5pt

(4.9)

Impressively, equations (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) compactly encode the number of independent colour-tensors involving
any
number of fundamental matter lines and adjoints at saturation for
any
of the classical Lie algebras in the limit of large-rank. The specific criteria for saturation for each of the classical Lie algebras is given in Table
6
below.

Table 6
:
Saturation points for scattering of
\mathbf{F}
-matter and adjoints for classical algebras.

It is important to note that while
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}<\,!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
, it is
not
true that
!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})\!<\,!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})
for all
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}
. This is perhaps startling: in
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
-type gauge theory the expectation would be that multi-traces over fundamental generators (which are counted combinatorially) should suffice to describe colour-structures involving fundamental matter and adjoints, but
this can’t be the whole story for the other classical Lie algebras
. If there were a combinatorial construction in terms of fundamental generators we would expect the same to apply to the other classical algebras: too many tensors due to (
e.g.
) dihedral symmetry amongst the traces is one thing, but not enough tensors is quite another. Since we may (for sufficiently large
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}
) have an excess,
!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})\!>!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})
,
multi-traces over fundamental generators alone can not be sufficient
. Analogous to the sub-saturation adjoint case for
\mathfrak{d}_{k}
discussed in section
3.2.2
, there are necessarily tensors which can not be expressed in terms of multi-traces of the type familiar from
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
. It is not clear whether or not these additional tensors are generated at the perturbative level in specific theories, nor how they fit into the particular-charge-representation-agnostic colour-tensors described by
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
(albeit, limited to tree-level). We must postpone such explorations to future work.

Below saturation, little can be said without wading into algebra-specific analyses. Broadly speaking though, the deviation from saturation mirrors the adjoint case:
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{k}}(\mathbf{F})

exceeds

!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q})
with the excess reducing (eventually to 0) as rank increases, and
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{c}_{k}}(\mathbf{F})<\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{b}_{k}}(\mathbf{F})
, both of which then saturate monotonically as rank increases, as may be seen from Figures
6
and
7
shown below. In
Figure
7
, we also show how increasing the number of fundamental particles has an effect very similar to shifting the counting in multiplicity.

Figure 6
:
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{F})
for each of the Lie algebras
\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}
.

Figure 7
:
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}}_{\mathfrak{d_{k}}}(\mathbf{F})
for Lie algebras
\mathfrak{d}_{k}
through rank
k{=}8
, and for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}\!\leq\!15
for
\mathfrak{d}_{5}
.

4.1.3
Scattering of Gluons and
Fundamental
Matter in
\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}
-Theories

It is not
a priori
clear that comparing the exceptional algebras to the classical ones at large rank is at all well motivated. In the case of scattering only adjoint matter, there was some expectation that an (over-)complete basis should exist of multi-trace tensors built from their fundamental representation’s generators, which would be of size
!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
for
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
and
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
for all the others. But when considering multiple numbers of charged matter, it is not at all clear there should exist a simple (over-)complete basis of symbols that make sense for all algebras. Nevertheless, in plotting these numbers, say, it is useful to compare to
something
which grows (nearly) as rapidly. For this reason, in
Figure
8
we have plotted
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{F})/(!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}))
to get some sense of their relative sizes.

Figure 8
:
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1}}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathbf{F})
for each of the exceptional Lie algebras
\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}
.

One of the unique things about
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
is that the fundamental
is
the adjoint,
\mathbf{248}
. Nevertheless, if we are considering adjoint
matter
this is different from the pure-adjoint analysis due to the breaking of Bose-symmetry. In Figure
8
we see familiar features: the exceptionals have an ‘excess’ at low-multiplicities (they exceed the expectation from the classical algebras) and then fall off at higher multiplicity, just as in Figure
5
. However, whereas in the adjoint case only
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
exceeded
!!{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
, all exceptional algebras with one fundamental-charged line exceed
!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1})
for some range of
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}

except
the case of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
. On the subject of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
, it is interesting to note that for
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{=}100
gluons, scattering with one fundamental-charged line involves
56
orders of magnitude fewer
colour tensors than the large-rank-saturated number
!!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}100},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1})
(which is itself merely 4 times fewer than the case of
!({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}100},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}1})
). It is perhaps unfair to read too deeply into this, however; after all, it is not clear that this is even close to an apples-to-apples comparison; but it suggests that is a dearth of interesting and ‘exceptional’ behaviour to be seen in the case of scattering matter in these gauge theories.

The above Figures are limited in that they only make the comparison for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}{=}1
. A more holistic comparison can be made from the data collected in Tables
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
, which give the specific values of
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{g}}
over modest ranges of adjoints and fundamental multiplicities.

Interestingly,
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{f}_{4}}(\mathbf{F})
has
far
more colour-tensors than any other algebra (besides
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
). In particular, it is smaller than
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}_{\mathfrak{e}_{7}}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}\!\leq\!22
. Despite this low-
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}
behavior, we expect on general grounds that all counting grows like a power of the dimension of the representation at large multiplicity; as such, we should not be surprised that the number of colours in
(\mathbf{56}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{56}})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}
for
\mathfrak{e}_{7}
eventually exceeds that of
(\mathbf{52}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{52}})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}
for
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
.

4.2
The Breakdown of the Large-
N_{c}
Limit for Some Charges of Matter

From the discussion thus far one may conclude that saturation is an inevitable feature for the large-rank limit of the classical algebras. This is however
not
the case. In the
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
case the saturation of gluons and fundamental matter follows simply from the recursive construction of the difference tables and the fact that the pure-adjoint case (empirically) saturates, but already this is somewhat mysterious and a perhaps spurious explanation, as for the other classical algebras saturation
does
occur despite the
lack
of a recursive construction.

A very simple prerequisite for ‘saturation’ of colour tensors to make sense in a theory with charged matter would be that

\lim_{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}\to\infty}m_{\mathfrak{g}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}k}}}\big((\mathbf{R}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{R}})^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}}\!\!\to\!\mathbf{1}\big)<\infty\vskip-0.5pt

(4.10)

for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}
. If this limit did not exist, then taking the rank to be arbitrarily-large would require an unbounded number of
new
independent colour-tensors for amplitudes involving
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}
matter lines charged in the representation
\mathbf{R}
.

From this point of view, it should be surprising that saturation exists for the fundamental representations of the classical Lie algebras. But as we have seen, these limits do empirically exist.

We suspect that a meaningful large-rank limit exists for all classical Lie algebras with matter charged under representations constructed from tensor-products of fundamental representations. But what about those representations
not
obtainable through tensor products of (what we have defined to be) the fundamental representations? There is only one kind of exception: the spinor representations of
\mathfrak{so}(n)
. It turns out that these
do not
saturate: we will see that

\begin{split}\text{\makebox[40.0pt][l]{$\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}}_{\mathfrak{b}_{k}}(\mathbf{S})$}}&=k{+}1\,\\
\text{\makebox[40.0pt][l]{$\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}0},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}}_{\mathfrak{d}_{k}}(\mathbf{S}_{\pm})$}}&=\lceil(k{+}1)/2\rceil\,;\end{split}\vskip-0.5pt

(4.11)

as such, no ‘large-
{N_{c}}
’ limit should exist for
\mathfrak{so}(k)
gauge theories for scattering amplitudes involving matter charged under spin-representations.

Amplitudes Involving Spinor Representations of
\mathfrak{b}_{k}
or
\mathfrak{d}_{k}

In
\mathfrak{d}_{k}
there are two
\mathbf{2^{k-1}}
-dimensional representations which are the two spinor representations
\mathbf{S}_{\pm}
. We can consider spinors of similar or different chirality coupled to adjoints. Specifically, we will consider
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{d}_{k}}[\mathbf{ad}^{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n},(\mathbf{S}_{\pm}\overline{\mathbf{S}_{\pm}})^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}]
. There is, however, a distinction for even and odd ranks we must address first. For
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}{=}1
and
k
even there is one
\mathbf{1}
in
\mathbf{S}_{\pm}^{2}
and none in
\mathbf{S}_{+}\otimes\mathbf{S}_{-}
, while for
k
odd there is one
\mathbf{1}
in
\mathbf{S}_{+}\otimes\mathbf{S}_{-}
and none in
\mathbf{S}_{\pm}^{2}
. This is reflecting the fact that
\overline{\mathbf{S}_{\pm}}\simeq\mathbf{S}_{\pm}
for
k
even and
\overline{\mathbf{S}_{\pm}}\simeq\mathbf{S}_{\mp}
for
k
odd: spinor representations are real for
k
even and complex for
k
odd. For two spinors and
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
gluons one saturates to
[A000985]
,
regardless
of rank.

Next, consider
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}q}{=}2
. Empirically, we find
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{d}_{k}}[(\mathbf{S}_{\pm}\overline{\mathbf{S}_{\pm}})^{2}]=\lceil(k{+}1)/2\rceil
, which evidently grows
linearly
with rank and will never saturate. We can understand this behaviour as follows (see
e.g.

[
41
]
for the required background). We denote
\psi_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{S}_{+}
,
\psi_{\alpha^{\prime}}\in\mathbf{S}_{-}
,
\psi^{\alpha}\in\overline{\mathbf{S}_{+}}
and
\psi^{\alpha^{\prime}}\in\overline{\mathbf{S}_{-}}
. For
k
even
\overline{\mathbf{S}_{\pm}}\simeq\mathbf{S}_{\pm}
and therefore
C_{\alpha\beta}\colon\overline{\mathbf{S}_{+}}\to\mathbf{S}_{+}
, which is sometimes called a
charge conjugation matrix
. We also have
C_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\colon\overline{\mathbf{S}_{-}}\to\mathbf{S}_{-}
. The Pauli matrices act as
(\sigma^{\mu})_{\alpha}{}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\colon\mathbf{S}_{-}\to\mathbf{S}_{+}
and
(\overline{\sigma}^{\mu})_{\alpha^{\prime}}{}^{\alpha}\colon\mathbf{S}_{+}\to\mathbf{S}_{-}
. A tensor product in
\mathbf{S}_{+}\otimes\mathbf{S}_{+}
has the tensor structure
M_{\alpha\beta}
and can be decomposed in a basis consisting of

C_{\alpha\beta},(\sigma^{[\mu_{1}}\overline{\sigma}^{\mu_{2}]}C)_{\alpha\beta},\cdots,(\sigma^{[\mu_{1}}\cdots\overline{\sigma}^{\mu_{k}]}C)_{\alpha\beta}.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.12)

These representations have dimensions
1,\binom{2k}{2},\dotsc,\frac{1}{2}\binom{2k}{k}
. The last representation above, the rank
k
form, has dimension
\frac{1}{2}\binom{2k}{k}
since a rank
k
form in a
2k
-dimensional space is reducible and can be decomposed in a self-dual and anti-self-dual irreducibles.

Since
\operatorname{dim}(\mathbf{S}_{\pm})=2^{k-1}
, we have

(2^{k-1})^{2}=1+\binom{2k}{2}+\cdots+\binom{2k}{2k-2}+\frac{1}{2}\binom{2k}{k}.

Indeed, this identity follows from
(1{+}1)^{2k}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{2k}\binom{2k}{n}
,
(1-1)^{2k}=\sum_{n=0}^{2k}({-}1)^{n}\binom{2k}{n}
and
\binom{2k}{n}=\binom{2k}{2k{-}n}
. Note that there are
\frac{k}{2}{+}1
terms in the decomposition of
\mathbf{S}_{+}\otimes\mathbf{S}_{+}
. We can obtain all the invariants (number of independent tensors) of
\mathbf{S}_{+}^{4}
by taking the decomposition of
\mathbf{S}_{+}^{2}
and contracting it with the decomposition of
\mathbf{S}_{+}^{2}
(there is only one way to do so). There are
\frac{k}{2}{+}1
terms in the decomposition which yield the same number of tensors.

If
k
is odd then we have
C_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\colon\overline{\mathbf{S}_{-}}\to\mathbf{S}_{+}
and
C_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\colon\overline{\mathbf{S}_{+}}\to\mathbf{S}_{-}
. Then, a tensor product
\mathbf{S}_{+}\otimes\mathbf{S}_{+}
with index structure
M_{\alpha\beta}
can be decomposed in a basis

(\sigma^{\mu_{1}}C)_{\alpha\beta},(\sigma^{[\mu_{1}}\overline{\sigma}^{\mu_{2}}\sigma^{\mu_{3}]}C)_{\alpha\beta},\dotsc,(\sigma^{[\mu_{1}}\overline{\sigma}^{\mu_{2}}\cdots\sigma^{\mu_{k}]}C)_{\alpha\beta}.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.13)

There are
\frac{k+1}{2}
terms in the decomposition. A similar counting holds for
\mathbf{S}_{-}\otimes\mathbf{S}_{-}
with the tensor structure
M^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
. It follows analogously that there are
\frac{k+1}{2}
invariants in
\mathbf{S}_{+}^{2}\mathbf{S}_{-}^{2}
. In this case we can also do the calculation by decomposing
(\mathbf{S}_{+}\otimes\mathbf{S}_{-})^{2}
by using the associativity of the tensor product. The tensor product
\mathbf{S}_{+}\otimes\mathbf{S}_{-}
can be decomposed in a basis of

C_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}},(\sigma^{[\mu_{1}}\overline{\sigma}^{\mu_{2}]}C)_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}},\dotsc,(\sigma^{[\mu_{1}}\cdots\overline{\sigma}^{\mu_{k-1}]}C)_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}.\vskip-0.5pt

(4.14)

We again have
\frac{k{+}1}{2}
independent tensors.

The above analysis was for
\mathfrak{d}_{k}
spinor representations, but a similar story holds for the
\mathbf{2^{k}}
(real) spinor representation of
\mathfrak{b}_{k}
. In this case we find
\mathcal{C}_{\mathfrak{b}_{k}}[\mathbf{S}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}2}]=k{+}1
, which again grows linearly with rank.

5
Open Problems for Future Work

In this work we have shown how representation theory algorithmically answers the question of how many colour-tensors appear in generic
\mathfrak{g}
-type gauge theory to all orders in perturbation theory. There are a number of refinements to the question which are deserving of follow-up.

5.1
Colour-Tensors Required in Perturbation Theory

We’ve already seen that for pure
\mathfrak{su}_{N}
gauge theory (and any other non-anomalous
\mathfrak{a}
-type gauge theory), this paper’s methodology over counts the number of independent colour-tensors. The kinematic coefficient to the
d^{abc}
tensor in pure Yang-Mills theory is zero to all loop-orders, and so despite there being two independent colour-tensors for
\mathfrak{a}_{k}

{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{=}3
only one appears in the theory. This mirrors the discussion of the deranged traces vs. dihedrally-deranged traces at 4-points in section
3.1
. One may wonder how many colour-tensors
actually appear
in a particular theory with specific matter content, where the counting herein should serve as an upper bound.

In the same vein, it would be extremely interesting to examine the
loop-dependence
on the number of colour-factors in a theory. At tree-level the DDM-basis is widely used and of size
(n{-}2)!
but because the asymptotics of our counting are
exponential
and not factorial, at some point the number of colour-tensors
to all-orders
will be fewer than the number in the DDM basis at
tree-level
! We expect that low loop-orders will carve out subsets of the number of possible tensors and at a certain loop-order one will have generated all of the allowed colour-tensors, matching the counting in this work. We have already confirmed that this is the case in
\mathfrak{su}_{2}
-type gauge theories, but more systematic study is needed. Studying that loop-dependence for different algebras, theories, and particle content is extremely interesting and will be a major part of our follow-up work.

5.2
Construction of Explicit Bases of Colour-Tensors

Bases of colour-tensors (perhaps over-complete) may be constructed in any number of ways: from traces of some single representation’s generators, from traces of multiple representations’ generators, from structure constants, etc. Each approach has its own advantages and disadvantages, but some of the weaknesses of the existing approaches motivate us to seek an alternative. First, the over-completeness of the bases should be remedied. The fact that the true size of the basis is unknown seems a remarkable gap in our understanding. Second, an orthogonalization of the tensors would prove immensely (and practically) useful. This would allow one to ‘project out’ gauge-invariant pieces of the amplitude trivially, amongst other desirable features. From a computational complexity perspective it reduces the calculation of colour-summed cross-sections substantially since there is no ‘colour-screening’, which for large multiplicity becomes significant; one need only consider the diagonal terms. Many of our methods are amenable to very high-multiplicity calculations, and it would be interesting to pursue this.

So far we have simply stated the benefits of an orthogonal and minimal basis, but a more representation theoretic critique is that it is not always obvious what objects should be used to construct the tensors. This is especially true when considering exotic matter. In the case of a trace basis, there is the perennial question of which representation’s generators we are using. And when is it possible to write all tensors in terms of traces of a
single
representation? These are not straightforward questions to answer and we wish to sidestep them entirely by having some more canonical way of generating tensors, something more inherent to the algebras and representations involved in a process.

A new basis is only as good as it is usable, so there will be a few challenges to address: we will have to detail how to construct the tensors in a way that is adaptable to any algebra and representation, we will have to describe how to project between the new and existing bases, and we will have to describe any interesting structure or relations satisfied by the tensors so-constructed (of which there will be an abundance!). That story goes well beyond the bounds of this present work, and so we delay further discussion of these interesting topics to
[
42
]
.

Appendix A
Multiplicities of Tensor Products’ Decompositions

Consider any simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of rank
k
with irreducible representations labelled by Dynkin indices
\vec{w}\!\in\!\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}
. For any three irreducible representations labelled by
\{\vec{w}_{a},\vec{w}_{b},\vec{w}_{c}\}
, let
m^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,c}
denote the
multiplicity
of the irreducible representation
\mathbf{c}
in the decomposition of the tensor product
\mathbf{a}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{b}
. That is,

\mathbf{a}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{b}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\mathbf{c}^{\oplus{m}^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,c}}\!\oplus\ldots\,\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\big({m}^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,c}\big)\mathbf{c}\oplus\ldots\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(A.1)

(Note that we have allowed ourselves the (very slight) abuse of notation:
r\,\mathbf{c}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\mathbf{c}^{\oplus r}
.) In terms of this, the complete tensor product decomposition of
\mathbf{a}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{b}
could be written

\mathbf{a}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{b}=\bigoplus_{c}m^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,c}\,\mathbf{c}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(A.2)

It is worth noting that this sum involves at most a finite number of terms.

Associativity of the tensor product allows us to write

\mathbf{a}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{b}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{c}=\big(\mathbf{a}\!\otimes\!\mathbf{b}\big)\!\otimes\!\mathbf{c}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}}\Big[\sum_{\ast}m^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,\ast}m^{\ast\,c}_{\phantom{\ast\,c}\,d}\Big]\mathbf{d}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip-4.0pt:\hskip 1.25pt}$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\bigoplus_{\mathbf{d}}m^{a\,b\,c}_{\phantom{a\,b\,c}\,d}\mathbf{d}\vskip-0.5pt

(A.3)

with the obvious generalization to an arbitrary number of indices.

The numbers
m^{a\cdots b}_{\phantom{a\cdots b}\,c}
is fully symmetric (by the definition of the tensor product) in
\{a,\ldots,b\}
but not generally symmetric under permutations involving the final index
c
. Let us define fully symmetric tensors

m^{a\cdots b}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}m^{a\cdots b}_{\phantom{a\cdots b}\,1}\,,\vskip-0.5pt

(A.4)

where we have used
\vec{w}_{1}\!=\![0,\ldots,0]
to denote the trivial representation. The two-index tensor is especially interesting:

m^{a\,b}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}m^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{$\;\;\;$}l}1&\mathbf{a}\!=\!\overline{\mathbf{b}}\\
0&\text{else}.\end{array}\right.\vskip-0.5pt

(A.5)

as it defines a non-degenerate bilinear form (a ‘
metric
’) on the space of irreducible representations, and can be used (in the physicists’ vernacular) to ‘raise/lower’ indices. Indeed, using (
A.3
) we see that

m^{a\,b\,c}=m^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,\ast}m^{\ast\,c}\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\sum_{\ast}m^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,\ast}m^{\ast\,c}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(A.6)

where we have now made use of the summation convention: repeated indices should always be summed (unless stated otherwise).

In this work, we have primarily been interested in the multiplicity of
\mathbf{1}
in the tensor products of representations relevant to coloured scattering amplitudes—namely,
\mathbf{ad}(\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\bigotimes_{i}(\mathbf{R}_{i}\!\otimes\!\overline{\mathbf{R}_{i}})^{\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m_{i}}}
which would encode the colour-dependence of amplitudes involving
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
gluons and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}m_{i}}
lines of Fermions transforming in the representation
\mathbf{R}_{i}
. The analysis above naturally suggests that the ways in which these numbers can be recursively computed in terms of just the fundamental three index objects
m^{a\,b}_{\phantom{a\,b}\,c}
—and in many different ways. However one chooses to recursively compute them, we end up with terms being counted by products of
m
’s, and summing over all the possible irreducible representations that connect them.

Such a sum is extremely reminiscent of the colour tensors generated by products of structure constants arising from the scattering of gluons. However, in our counting game, we always end with a trivalent
tree
graph—with the edges labeling irreducible representations of the algebra.

Associating these with tensors constructed from the actual representations involved turns out to immediately translate this counting exercise into an
explicit construction of independent tensors
. These tensors turn out to have many remarkable properties (such as orthogonality), as we describe in the forthcoming work
[
42
]
.

Appendix B
Decomposing Tensor Products into Irreducible Reps

To decompose the tensor products into irreducible representations we can use the Racah-Speiser algorithm
11
11

11

Sometimes also attributed to Brauer, Klimyk and Steinberg (see ref.
[
43
]
).
(see ref.
[
44
,
45
]
), which works as follows. Consider two representations
R_{\Lambda_{1}}
and
R_{\Lambda_{2}}
with highest weights
\Lambda_{1}
,
\Lambda_{2}
. Then we have

R_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes R_{\Lambda_{2}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\operatorname{wt}R_{\Lambda_{2}}}R_{\Lambda_{1}+\lambda}\vskip-0.5pt

(B.1)

and the sum is over the weights of the representation
R_{\Lambda_{2}}
including multiplicities; the same construction can be done by swapping
R_{\Lambda_{1}}
and
R_{\Lambda_{2}}
.

Sometimes when adding
\Lambda_{1}+\lambda
we do not obtain a valid highest weight since some of the entries of the weight vector become negative. In that case the algorithm instructs us to reflect by an element of the Weyl group such that the entries are positive. For such representations we have

R_{\Lambda}=\operatorname{sign}(w)R_{w\cdot\Lambda},\vskip-0.5pt

(B.2)

for
w
in the Weyl group of the Lie algebra. The dot action is defined by

w\cdot\Lambda=w(\Lambda{+}\rho){-}\rho,\vskip-0.5pt

(B.3)

where
\rho
is the Weyl vector

\rho=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha>0}\alpha,\vskip-0.5pt

(B.4)

which is the sum of positive roots.

We illustrate the procedure on a few examples.
\mathfrak{su}_{3}
has Cartan matrix

C\text{\makebox[14.5pt][c]{$\hskip-150.0pt\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\text{\makebox[0.0pt][r]{$\raisebox{0.47pt}{\hskip 1.25pt:\hskip-4.0pt}$}}=\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$$}}\hskip-150.0pt$}}\,\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\
-1&2\end{pmatrix}\vskip-0.5pt

(B.5)

and a reflection
w_{i}
acts via

w_{i}[\dotsc,\lambda_{j},\dotsc]=[\dotsc,\lambda_{j}{-}\lambda_{i}C_{ij},\dotsc].\vskip-0.5pt

(B.6)

For the adjoint we have

\operatorname{wt}(\mathbf{ad})=\{[1,1],[-1,2],[2,-1],[0,0]^{2},[1,-2],[-2,1],[-1,-1]\},\vskip-0.5pt

(B.7)

where the weight
[0,0]
occurs with multiplicity 2.

So the calculation we need to do is to start with some dots in the positive Weyl chamber (which are the vectors with positive Dynkin labels) and start with initial condition that the weight at
[1,1]
has multiplicity one and the rest zero. Then add all the weights of the adjoint to it and reflect (or fold over) in the hyperplanes orthogonal to
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
and passing through
-\rho
where
\rho
is the Weyl vector. If we do an odd number of reflections, subtract the multiplicities otherwise add. Repeat as many times as necessary.

We have
\mathbf{ad}=R_{[1,1]}
. Let us apply the Racah-Speiser algorithm to decomposing
\mathbf{ad}\otimes\mathbf{ad}
. We need to compute
R_{[1,1]}\otimes R_{[1,1]}
. Adding all the weights of
R_{[1,1]}
we find

R_{[1,1]}\otimes R_{[1,1]}=R_{[2,2]}\oplus R_{[0,3]}\oplus R_{[3,0]}\oplus R_{[1,1]}^{\oplus 2}\oplus R_{[-1,2]}\oplus R_{[2,-1]}\oplus R_{[0,0]}.\vskip-0.5pt

(B.8)

The weights
[-1,2]
and
[2,-1]
are outside the positive Weyl chamber so we need to reflect them. Since
s_{1}\cdot[-1,2]=[-1,2]
so we have
R_{[-1,2]}=-R_{[-1,2]}
which implies that
R_{[-1,2]}=0
. Similarly,
R_{[2,-1]}=0
. In the end, we have

R_{[1,1]}\otimes R_{[1,1]}=R_{[2,2]}\oplus R_{[0,3]}\oplus R_{[3,0]}\oplus R_{[1,1]}^{\oplus 2}\oplus R_{[0,0]}.\vskip-0.5pt

(B.9)

The root system of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
can be conveniently described inside
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We have the following roots
\pm(e_{i}-e_{j})
for
i<j
(short roots) and
\pm(2e_{i}-e_{j}-e_{k})
for
i,j,k
all different (long roots).

The simple roots are
\alpha_{1}=e_{1}-e_{2}
and
\alpha_{2}=-2e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}
. We have
\alpha_{1}^{2}=2
,
\alpha_{2}^{2}=6
and
\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}=-3
. The Cartan matrix is

C_{ij}=\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{j}^{\vee}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\
-3&2\end{pmatrix}.\vskip-0.5pt

(B.10)

The six positive roots can be written in terms of the simple roots as follows

\displaystyle e_{1}-e_{2}=\alpha_{1},

(B.11)

\displaystyle e_{3}-e_{1}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},

(B.12)

\displaystyle e_{3}-e_{2}=2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},

(B.13)

\displaystyle 2e_{1}-e_{2}-e_{3}=\alpha_{2},

(B.14)

\displaystyle-(2e_{2}-e_{1}-e_{3})=3\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},

(B.15)

\displaystyle 2e_{3}-e_{1}-e_{2}=3\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}.

(B.16)

In terms of fundamental weights we have

\alpha_{1}=2\omega_{1}{-}\omega_{2},\\
\alpha_{2}={-}3\omega_{1}{+}2\omega_{2}.\vskip-0.5pt

(B.17)

The highest root is

\theta=3\alpha_{1}{+}2\alpha_{2}=\omega_{2}.\vskip-0.5pt

(B.18)

In this basis the positive roots above read

\displaystyle e_{1}-e_{2}=\alpha_{1}=2\omega_{1}-\omega_{2},

(B.19)

\displaystyle e_{3}-e_{1}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=-\omega_{1}+\omega_{2},

(B.20)

\displaystyle e_{3}-e_{2}=2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=\omega_{1},

(B.21)

\displaystyle 2e_{1}-e_{2}-e_{3}=\alpha_{2}=-3\omega_{1}+2\omega_{2},

(B.22)

\displaystyle-(2e_{2}-e_{1}-e_{3})=3\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}=3\omega_{1}-\omega_{2},

(B.23)

\displaystyle 2e_{3}-e_{1}-e_{2}=3\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}=\omega_{2}.

(B.24)

The weights in the adjoint representation are therefore
[2,-1]
,
[-1,1]
,
[1,0]
,
[-3,2]
,
[3,-1]
,
[0,1]
,
[0,0]
twice and then the negatives of the above.

Then, the Racah-Speiser theorem implies that

[0,1]\otimes[0,1]=[2,0]\oplus[-1,2]\oplus[1,1]\oplus[-3,3]\oplus[3,0]\oplus[0,2]\oplus[0,1]^{\oplus 2}\oplus[-2,2]\oplus\\
[1,0]\oplus[-1,1]\oplus[3,-1]\oplus[-3,2]\oplus[0,0].

(B.25)

We can immediately drop the terms
[-1,2]
,
[-1,1]
and
[3,-1]
. Next, we need to reflect
[-3,3]
,
[-2,2]
and
[-3,2]
. We have

\displaystyle s_{1}\cdot[-3,3]=s_{1}([-2,4])-[1,1]=[2,2]-[1,1]=[1,1],

(B.26)

\displaystyle s_{1}\cdot[-2,2]=s_{1}([-1,3])-[1,1]=[1,2]-[1,1]=[0,1],

(B.27)

\displaystyle s_{1}\cdot[-3,2]=s_{1}([-2,3])-[1,1]=[2,1]-[1,1]=[1,0].

(B.28)

Here we have used the fact that

\displaystyle s_{1}([\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}])=[-\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}],

(B.29)

\displaystyle s_{2}([\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}])=[\lambda_{1}+3\lambda_{2},-\lambda_{2}].

(B.30)

In then end we have

[0,1]\otimes[0,1]=[2,0]\oplus[1,1]\ominus[1,1]\oplus[3,0]\oplus[0,2]\oplus[0,1]^{\oplus 2}\ominus[0,1]\oplus[1,0]\ominus[1,0]\oplus[0,0]\vskip-0.5pt

(B.31)

which simplifies to

[0,1]\otimes[0,1]=[2,0]\oplus[3,0]\oplus[0,2]\oplus[0,1]\oplus[0,0].\vskip-0.5pt

(B.32)

Appendix D
Asymptotic Growth of Colour-Factors for Large
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}

In ref.
[
46
]
, Biane has computed the asymptotic expansion in
n
of the multiplicity of
R_{\mu}
in the decomposition of
R_{\lambda}^{\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}
. In ref.
[
47
]
, theorem 8, a more detailed explanation of the terms in Biane’s formula is given. Ref.
[
47
]
uses the notation
A
for the Biane’s quadratic form
q
. We adopt their notation below. We have

A_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{\operatorname{dim}R_{\lambda}}\sum_{\mu\in\operatorname{wt}(R_{\lambda})}m_{\mu}\mu\otimes\mu,\vskip-0.5pt

(D.1)

where
R_{\lambda}
is the irreducible representation with highest weight
\lambda
,
\operatorname{wt}(R_{\lambda})
is the set of weights of the representation
R_{\lambda}
,
m_{\mu}
is the multiplicity of the weight
\mu
. We denote by
P
the weight lattice. Since
\mu\!\in\!P\!\subset\!\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, we can think of it as a map
\mathfrak{h}^{*}\!\otimes\!\mathfrak{h}^{*}\!\to\!\mathbb{C}
or, in matrix form, as a linear map
\mathfrak{h}^{*}\!\to\!\mathfrak{h}
. We can express the weights in a basis of fundamental weights
\omega
with integer coefficients:

A_{\lambda}=\sum_{i,j}(A_{\lambda})_{ij}\omega_{i}\otimes\omega_{j}\vskip-0.5pt

(D.2)

where

(A_{\lambda})_{ij}=\frac{1}{\operatorname{dim}R_{\lambda}}\sum_{\mu\in\operatorname{wt}(R_{\lambda})}m_{\mu}\mu(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})\mu(\alpha_{j}^{\vee}).\vskip-0.5pt

(D.3)

For the inverse we have

A^{-1}_{\lambda}=\sum_{k,l}(A^{-1}_{\lambda})_{kl}\alpha_{k}^{\vee}\otimes\alpha_{l}^{\vee}.\vskip-0.5pt

(D.4)

Then, we have

\text{mult}(R_{\mu},R_{\lambda}^{\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}})=\frac{\lvert\Pi(G)\rvert(\operatorname{dim}R_{\lambda})^{n}(\operatorname{dim}R_{\mu})\prod_{\alpha>0}\langle A_{\lambda}^{-1}\alpha,\rho\rangle}{\sqrt{\det A_{\lambda}}(2\pi)^{\text{rank}G/2}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{\operatorname{dim}G/2}}\\
\Bigl(\exp(-\langle A_{\lambda}^{-1}(\mu+\rho),\mu+\rho\rangle/(2{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}))+\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{-1})\Bigr),

(D.5)

where
\Pi(G)
is the determinant of the Cartan matrix of the Lie algebra of
G
.

For
\lambda{=}\theta
, when we are dealing with the adjoint representation, we have

(A_{\theta})_{ij}=\frac{1}{\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{g}}\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}\alpha(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})\alpha(\alpha_{j}^{\vee}).\vskip-0.5pt

(D.6)

We know that (see ref.
[
20
, eqs. 13.174, 13.184]
)

(\lambda,\mu)=\frac{1}{2g^{\vee}}\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta}(\lambda,\alpha)(\alpha,\mu),\vskip-0.5pt

(D.7)

where
g^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number.
12
12

12

The dual Coxeter number is defined as follows. If
\theta
is the highest root, then decompose it as
\theta=\sum_{i}a_{i}^{\vee}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
and
g^{\vee}:=1+\sum_{i}a_{i}^{\vee}
. The Coxeter number is
g:=1+\sum_{i}a_{i}
where
\theta=\sum_{i}a_{i}\alpha_{i}
.
Using this we find

(A_{\theta})_{ij}=\frac{2g^{\vee}}{\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha_{i}^{\vee},\alpha_{j}^{\vee}).\vskip-0.5pt

(D.8)

Since
\omega_{i}
is the dual basis to
\alpha^{\vee}_{j}
, we have that

(A_{\theta}^{-1})_{ij}=\frac{\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{g}}{2g^{\vee}}(\omega_{i},\omega_{j}).\vskip-0.5pt

(D.9)

If
\lambda{=}\theta
and
\mu{=}0
we have for the quantity in the exponent

\langle A_{\lambda}^{-1}(\mu{+}\rho),\mu{+}\rho\rangle=(A_{\theta}^{-1})_{ij}\rho_{i}\rho_{j}=\frac{\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{g}}{2g^{\vee}}\lvert\rho\rvert^{2}.\vskip-0.5pt

(D.10)

Next, we can use the Freudenthal-de Vries strange formula (see refs.
[
48
]
,
[
20
, eq. 13.179]
)

\lvert\rho\rvert^{2}=\frac{\hskip-1.0pt\phantom{{}^{\vee}\hskip-1.0pt}g^{\vee}}{12}\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{g}.\vskip-0.5pt

(D.11)

It follows that the quantity in the exponent is

\frac{(\operatorname{dim}\mathfrak{g})^{2}}{48{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}.\vskip-0.5pt

(D.12)

From Biane’s formula the asymptotic behavior have two terms,

\exp\!\left(-\frac{\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})^{2}}{48{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\right)+\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{-1})\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(D.13)

The leading term (the exponential) will then dominate the sub-leading terms for

{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\log({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})\gg\frac{\mathrm{dim}(\mathfrak{g})^{2}}{48}\,.\vskip-0.5pt

(D.14)

Algebras of smaller dimensions will evidently converge to the (leading term in the) asymptotic formulae for lower
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}
than for algebras of larger dimension.

D.1
\mathfrak{a}
-Type Gauge Theory

\begin{split}\text{\makebox[40.0pt][r]{$\mathfrak{a}_{1}\!\!:\,\,\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}$}}&\hskip 4.0pt\text{\makebox[300.0pt][l]{$\left(\hskip-2.0pt\frac{1\hskip-4.0pt}{2^{\hskip-0.25pt3}\!\,\,}\sqrt{\hskip-2.0pt\frac{3}{\pi}\hskip-2.0pt}\hskip-4.0pt\hskip 5.0pt\right)\hskip-2.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}^{\hskip-0.25pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\text{+}1}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}}{2}}\exp\!\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}^{2}\hskip-3.0pt}{\hskip 1.0pt48\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\,\right\}\Big(\!1{+}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\right)\!\!\Big);$}}\\

(D.15)

In the case of
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
an extra sub-leading term is known if we also expand the exponential in the limit
n\!\to\!\infty
. Indeed, we have (see refs.
[
25
,
49
]
)

\mathcal{C}\big[\mathbf{ad}(\mathfrak{a}_{1})^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\big]\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}\frac{3^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}+2}}{\sqrt{3{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\pi}8{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}(1-\frac{21}{16{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}+\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{-2})).

(D.16)

To the same order in
{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{-1}
this can be rewritten as

\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}{+}1}\exp\!\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}3}^{2}\hskip-3.0pt}{\hskip 1.0pt48\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\,\right\}\Big(1{-}\frac{9}{8\,{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\Big)\Big(1{+}\mathcal{O}\!\big(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{2}\big)\Big)\,.

(D.17)

While the leading order formula matches the data well for
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
for other algebras (of larger dimension) the convergence of data to the asymptotic formula will happen at some (potentially large) multiplicity. Other examples of
\mathcal{O}(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n})
obtained by direct comparison with ‘data’ at large multiplicity were given in (
3.29
).

(D.18)

D.3

(D.21)

\mathfrak{g}_{2}\!\!:\,\,\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}_{\mathfrak{g}_{2}}\mathrel{\overset{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\to\infty$}}{\resizebox{1296991}{0}{$\sim$}}}

\left(\hskip-2.0pt\frac{5\hskip-2.0pt\cdot\hskip-3.0pt7^{\hskip-0.25pt5}\!\hskip-4.0pt}{2^{\hskip-0.25pt14}\!\hskip-2.0pt\cdot\hskip-3.0pt3^{\hskip-0.25pt3}\!\,\,\pi}\sqrt{\hskip-2.0pt\frac{1}{3}\hskip-2.0pt}\hskip-4.0pt\hskip 5.0pt\right)\hskip-2.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}14}^{\hskip-0.25pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\text{+}2}{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}^{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}14}}{2}}\exp\!\left\{\hskip-0.5pt\text{--}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.575}14}^{2}\hskip-3.0pt}{\hskip 1.0pt48\hskip 1.0pt{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}}\,\right\}\Big(\!1{+}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1/{\color[rgb]{0.575,0,0.225}n}\right)\!\!\Big).

The numerical prefactors are complicated, but their factorization has some interesting features. For example, for
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
it contains
1
,
7
,
11
,
13
,
17
,
19
,
23
,
29
which are the exponents of
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
. The exponents are the integers
n_{i}
such that the Cartan matrix has
4\cos^{2}\frac{\pi n_{i}}{2g^{\vee}}
as eigenvalues, where
g^{\vee}
is the dual Coxeter number. For
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
the Coxeter number is
30
and we also have the prime number
g+1=31
in the prefactor (see Table
17
). For the non-simply-laced algebras the Coxeter number and the dual Coxeter numbers are different.

\mathfrak{c}_{k}

1,3,\dotsc,2k-1

2k

\mathfrak{d}_{k}

1,3,\dotsc,2k-3,k-1

:
Indices, Coxeter number
g
and dual Coxeter number

for simple Lie algebras.
